Even when I write things down, I’ll still miss them. I’ve forgotten to return calls, or I’ll find a bill sitting under a pile of mail that I meant to pay. I’ve forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s frustrating, because I really do mean to take care of these things.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you describe how long you’ve been experiencing these difficulties with focus and organization? Sometimes it’s a recipe I need for the church supper, or the folder I’m supposed to take to the doctor’s office, and I’ll be hunting all over the house for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things like appointments, conversations, or tasks you had planned to do, even if they’re written down?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I write things down, I’ll still miss them. I’ve forgotten to return calls, or I’ll find a bill sitting under a pile of mail that I meant to pay. I’ve forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s frustrating, because I really do mean to take care of these things.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you’ve been experiencing these difficulties with focus and organization? Have they been present for most of your life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been this bad for about a year and three months, I’d say. It really started causing trouble when my husband began needing regular help with his appointments and day-to-day arrangements. Before that, I managed better, but since then it’s been much harder to keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you, like your husband or family members, noticed changes in your memory or thinking abilities that concern them?\n\nPatient: Yes. My daughter has noticed, for sure. She’s started double-checking the bills and appointments because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep them all straight by myself anymore. I’ve forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s frustrating, because I really do mean to take care of these things.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you’ve been experiencing these difficulties with focus and organization? Have they been present for most of your life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been this bad for about a year and three months, I’d say. It really started causing trouble when my husband began needing regular help with his appointments and day-to-day arrangements. Before that, I managed better, but since then it’s been much harder to keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you, like your husband or family members, noticed changes in your memory or thinking abilities that concern them?\n\nPatient: Yes. My daughter has noticed, for sure. She’s started double-checking the bills and appointments because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep them all straight by myself anymore. And I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center, which was hard. I just couldn’t stay on top of all the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused or organized when you’re outside the house, like when you’re running errands, attending events, or talking with friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If I’m at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my attention just goes to them, even if I’m trying to write something down or plan something. Same thing at home if the television is on in the next room. I lose my place, and then I have to figure out what I was doing all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you can’t sit still for very long?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do notice that. Even when I’m trying to sit still and be polite in a meeting, I’ll find myself rubbing my hands, tapping my foot, clicking a pen, or shifting around in my chair. Sometimes I realize I’m doing it because people look over at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they’re talking, finishing their sentences, or acting on impulse without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as interrupting people a lot, but maybe I do it once in a while and don’t notice. Acting without thinking… I can’t say that’s a big thing I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have trouble staying focused or felt unusually restless or fidgety when you were a child, say before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember even when I was little, my teachers would say I was bright, but careless. They wrote that I forgot my books or homework, and I needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So yes, it was there when I was a child.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll have my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists all over the kitchen table. I sit down meaning to get it organized, and I just don’t know what to do first. Then I get stuck there, and somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused or organized in other parts of your life, like remembering conversations, keeping track of personal items, or following through with plans?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with keeping track of things. I’m forever looking for something I just had in my hand—my car keys, my reading glasses, appointment cards, that sort of thing. Sometimes it’s a recipe I need for the church supper, or the folder I’m supposed to take to the doctor’s office, and I’ll be hunting all over the house for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things like appointments, conversations, or tasks you had planned to do, even if they’re written down?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I write things down, I’ll still miss them. I’ve forgotten to return calls, or I’ll find a bill sitting under a pile of mail that I meant to pay. I’ve forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s frustrating, because I really do mean to take care of these things.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you’ve been experiencing these difficulties with focus and organization? Have they been present for most of your life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been this bad for about a year and three months, I’d say. It really started causing trouble when my husband began needing regular help with his appointments and day-to-day arrangements. Before that, I managed better, but since then it’s been much harder to keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you, like your husband or family members, noticed changes in your memory or thinking abilities that concern them?\n\nPatient: Yes. My daughter has noticed, for sure. She’s started double-checking the bills and appointments because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep them all straight by myself anymore. And I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center, which was hard. I just couldn’t stay on top of all the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused or organized when you’re outside the house, like when you’re running errands, attending events, or talking with friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If I’m at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my attention just goes to them, even if I’m trying to write something down or plan something. Same thing at home if the television is on in the next room. I lose my place, and then I have to figure out what I was doing all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you can’t sit still for very long?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do notice that. Even when I’m trying to sit still and be polite in a meeting, I’ll find myself rubbing my hands, tapping my foot, clicking a pen, or shifting around in my chair. Sometimes I realize I’m doing it because people look over at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they’re talking, finishing their sentences, or acting on impulse without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as interrupting people a lot, but maybe I do it once in a while and don’t notice. Acting without thinking… I can’t say that’s a big thing I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have trouble staying focused or felt unusually restless or fidgety when you were a child, say before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember even when I was little, my teachers would say I was bright, but careless. They wrote that I forgot my books or homework, and I needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So yes, it was there when I was a child.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a steady decline in your thinking or memory abilities over time, or do the difficulties come and go but have gotten worse recently?\n\nPatient: It’s not like I noticed a steady decline for years and years. It’s been this bad for about a year and three months now, since my husband started needing regular help with his appointments and just the day-to-day arrangements. That’s when it really started causing me a lot more trouble.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "question": "Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking, like trouble with judgment, problem-solving, or understanding visual information, such as getting lost in familiar places or having trouble recognizing objects?"

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. My kitchen table is piled up with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I sit down to sort it out, but I don’t know what to do first, and then somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to organize tasks or complete everyday activities like paying bills, planning meals, or keeping track of your schedule?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all these papers—my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and little half-finished lists—and I don’t know what to handle first. I’ll mean to sort through it, but then I get stuck, and before I know it, the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning or organizing tasks in other areas of your life, like managing your schedule or completing projects at work or home?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s mostly at home now, since I’m not working, but it shows up with anything I have to plan or sort out. I’ll have my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists all over the kitchen table. I sit down meaning to get it organized, and I just don’t know what to do first. Then I get stuck there, and somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused or organized in other parts of your life, like remembering conversations, keeping track of personal items, or following through with plans?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with keeping track of things. I’m forever looking for something I just had in my hand—my car keys, my reading glasses, appointment cards, that sort of thing. Sometimes it’s a recipe I need for the church supper, or the folder I’m supposed to take to the doctor’s office, and I’ll be hunting all over the house for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things like appointments, conversations, or tasks you had planned to do, even if they’re written down?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I write things down, I’ll still miss them. I’ve forgotten to return calls, or I’ll find a bill sitting under a pile of mail that I meant to pay. I’ve forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s frustrating, because I really do mean to take care of these things.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you’ve been experiencing these difficulties with focus and organization? Have they been present for most of your life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been this bad for about a year and three months, I’d say. It really started causing trouble when my husband began needing regular help with his appointments and day-to-day arrangements. Before that, I managed better, but since then it’s been much harder to keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you, like your husband or family members, noticed changes in your memory or thinking abilities that concern them?\n\nPatient: Yes. My daughter has noticed, for sure. She’s started double-checking the bills and appointments because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep them all straight by myself anymore. And I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center, which was hard. I just couldn’t stay on top of all the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused or organized when you’re outside the house, like when you’re running errands, attending events, or talking with friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If I’m at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my attention just goes to them, even if I’m trying to write something down or plan something. Same thing at home if the television is on in the next room. I lose my place, and then I have to figure out what I was doing all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you can’t sit still for very long?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do notice that. Even when I’m trying to sit still and be polite in a meeting, I’ll find myself rubbing my hands, tapping my foot, clicking a pen, or shifting around in my chair. Sometimes I realize I’m doing it because people look over at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they’re talking, finishing their sentences, or acting on impulse without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as interrupting people a lot, but maybe I do it once in a while and don’t notice. Acting without thinking… I can’t say that’s a big thing I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have trouble staying focused or felt unusually restless or fidgety when you were a child, say before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember even when I was little, my teachers would say I was bright, but careless. They wrote that I forgot my books or homework, and I needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So yes, it was there when I was a child.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a steady decline in your thinking or memory abilities over time, or do the difficulties come and go but have gotten worse recently?\n\nPatient: It’s not like I noticed a steady decline for years and years. It’s been this bad for about a year and three months now, since my husband started needing regular help with his appointments and just the day-to-day arrangements. That’s when it really started causing me a lot more trouble.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking, like trouble with judgment, problem-solving, or understanding visual information, such as getting lost in familiar places or having trouble recognizing objects?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I don’t think I’m getting lost in places I know, or having trouble recognizing things. Judgment or problem-solving… I’m not sure, but nothing stands out to me in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to follow through with tasks that require sustained mental effort, like reading a book, working on a puzzle, or planning something in detail?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, or even putting together a schedule for something, I’ll keep finding little reasons to put it off. It feels like my head is tired before I even start, so I just avoid it as long as I can.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.9, F33.9, F06.7, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. My kitchen table is piled up with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I sit down to sort it out, but I don’t know what to do first, and then somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to organize tasks or complete everyday activities like paying bills, planning meals, or keeping track of your schedule?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all these papers—my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and little half-finished lists—and I don’t know what to handle first. I’ll mean to sort through it, but then I get stuck, and before I know it, the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning or organizing tasks in other areas of your life, like managing your schedule or completing projects at work or home?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s mostly at home now, since I’m not working, but it shows up with anything I have to plan or sort out. I’ll have my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists all over the kitchen table. I sit down meaning to get it organized, and I just don’t know what to do first. Then I get stuck there, and somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused or organized in other parts of your life, like remembering conversations, keeping track of personal items, or following through with plans?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with keeping track of things. I’m forever looking for something I just had in my hand—my car keys, my reading glasses, appointment cards, that sort of thing. Sometimes it’s a recipe I need for the church supper, or the folder I’m supposed to take to the doctor’s office, and I’ll be hunting all over the house for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things like appointments, conversations, or tasks you had planned to do, even if they’re written down?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I write things down, I’ll still miss them. I’ve forgotten to return calls, or I’ll find a bill sitting under a pile of mail that I meant to pay. I’ve forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s frustrating, because I really do mean to take care of these things.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you’ve been experiencing these difficulties with focus and organization? Have they been present for most of your life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been this bad for about a year and three months, I’d say. It really started causing trouble when my husband began needing regular help with his appointments and day-to-day arrangements. Before that, I managed better, but since then it’s been much harder to keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you, like your husband or family members, noticed changes in your memory or thinking abilities that concern them?\n\nPatient: Yes. My daughter has noticed, for sure. She’s started double-checking the bills and appointments because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep them all straight by myself anymore. And I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center, which was hard. I just couldn’t stay on top of all the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused or organized when you’re outside the house, like when you’re running errands, attending events, or talking with friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If I’m at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my attention just goes to them, even if I’m trying to write something down or plan something. Same thing at home if the television is on in the next room. I lose my place, and then I have to figure out what I was doing all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you can’t sit still for very long?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do notice that. Even when I’m trying to sit still and be polite in a meeting, I’ll find myself rubbing my hands, tapping my foot, clicking a pen, or shifting around in my chair. Sometimes I realize I’m doing it because people look over at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they’re talking, finishing their sentences, or acting on impulse without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as interrupting people a lot, but maybe I do it once in a while and don’t notice. Acting without thinking… I can’t say that’s a big thing I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have trouble staying focused or felt unusually restless or fidgety when you were a child, say before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember even when I was little, my teachers would say I was bright, but careless. They wrote that I forgot my books or homework, and I needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So yes, it was there when I was a child.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a steady decline in your thinking or memory abilities over time, or do the difficulties come and go but have gotten worse recently?\n\nPatient: It’s not like I noticed a steady decline for years and years. It’s been this bad for about a year and three months now, since my husband started needing regular help with his appointments and just the day-to-day arrangements. That’s when it really started causing me a lot more trouble.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking, like trouble with judgment, problem-solving, or understanding visual information, such as getting lost in familiar places or having trouble recognizing objects?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I don’t think I’m getting lost in places I know, or having trouble recognizing things. Judgment or problem-solving… I’m not sure, but nothing stands out to me in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to follow through with tasks that require sustained mental effort, like reading a book, working on a puzzle, or planning something in detail?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, or even putting together a schedule for something, I’ll keep finding little reasons to put it off. It feels like my head is tired before I even start, so I just avoid it as long as I can.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your difficulties with focus and organization affect your daily routines, such as managing your time, starting tasks, or staying on track with responsibilities?\n\nPatient: It affects almost everything, honestly. I came in because I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and all these half-finished lists. I’ll sit down to try to sort it out, and I just don’t know which thing to do first. Then I end up sitting there or moving papers around, and before I know it, the whole day is gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My kitchen table is piled up with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I sit down to sort it out, but I don’t know what to do first, and then somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to organize tasks or complete everyday activities like paying bills, planning meals, or keeping track of your schedule?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all these papers—my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and little half-finished lists—and I don’t know what to handle first. I’ll mean to sort through it, but then I get stuck, and before I know it, the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning or organizing tasks in other areas of your life, like managing your schedule or completing projects at work or home?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s mostly at home now, since I’m not working, but it shows up with anything I have to plan or sort out. I’ll have my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists all over the kitchen table. I sit down meaning to get it organized, and I just don’t know what to do first. Then I get stuck there, and somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused or organized in other parts of your life, like remembering conversations, keeping track of personal items, or following through with plans?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with keeping track of things. I’m forever looking for something I just had in my hand—my car keys, my reading glasses, appointment cards, that sort of thing. Sometimes it’s a recipe I need for the church supper, or the folder I’m supposed to take to the doctor’s office, and I’ll be hunting all over the house for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things like appointments, conversations, or tasks you had planned to do, even if they’re written down?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I write things down, I’ll still miss them. I’ve forgotten to return calls, or I’ll find a bill sitting under a pile of mail that I meant to pay. I’ve forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s frustrating, because I really do mean to take care of these things.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you’ve been experiencing these difficulties with focus and organization? Have they been present for most of your life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been this bad for about a year and three months, I’d say. It really started causing trouble when my husband began needing regular help with his appointments and day-to-day arrangements. Before that, I managed better, but since then it’s been much harder to keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you, like your husband or family members, noticed changes in your memory or thinking abilities that concern them?\n\nPatient: Yes. My daughter has noticed, for sure. She’s started double-checking the bills and appointments because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep them all straight by myself anymore. And I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center, which was hard. I just couldn’t stay on top of all the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused or organized when you’re outside the house, like when you’re running errands, attending events, or talking with friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If I’m at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my attention just goes to them, even if I’m trying to write something down or plan something. Same thing at home if the television is on in the next room. I lose my place, and then I have to figure out what I was doing all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you can’t sit still for very long?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do notice that. Even when I’m trying to sit still and be polite in a meeting, I’ll find myself rubbing my hands, tapping my foot, clicking a pen, or shifting around in my chair. Sometimes I realize I’m doing it because people look over at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they’re talking, finishing their sentences, or acting on impulse without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as interrupting people a lot, but maybe I do it once in a while and don’t notice. Acting without thinking… I can’t say that’s a big thing I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have trouble staying focused or felt unusually restless or fidgety when you were a child, say before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember even when I was little, my teachers would say I was bright, but careless. They wrote that I forgot my books or homework, and I needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So yes, it was there when I was a child.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a steady decline in your thinking or memory abilities over time, or do the difficulties come and go but have gotten worse recently?\n\nPatient: It’s not like I noticed a steady decline for years and years. It’s been this bad for about a year and three months now, since my husband started needing regular help with his appointments and just the day-to-day arrangements. That’s when it really started causing me a lot more trouble.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking, like trouble with judgment, problem-solving, or understanding visual information, such as getting lost in familiar places or having trouble recognizing objects?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I don’t think I’m getting lost in places I know, or having trouble recognizing things. Judgment or problem-solving… I’m not sure, but nothing stands out to me in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to follow through with tasks that require sustained mental effort, like reading a book, working on a puzzle, or planning something in detail?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. My kitchen table is piled up with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I sit down to sort it out, but I don’t know what to do first, and then somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to organize tasks or complete everyday activities like paying bills, planning meals, or keeping track of your schedule?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all these papers—my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and little half-finished lists—and I don’t know what to handle first. I’ll mean to sort through it, but then I get stuck, and before I know it, the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning or organizing tasks in other areas of your life, like managing your schedule or completing projects at work or home?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s mostly at home now, since I’m not working, but it shows up with anything I have to plan or sort out. I’ll have my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists all over the kitchen table. I sit down meaning to get it organized, and I just don’t know what to do first. Then I get stuck there, and somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused or organized in other parts of your life, like remembering conversations, keeping track of personal items, or following through with plans?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with keeping track of things. I’m forever looking for something I just had in my hand—my car keys, my reading glasses, appointment cards, that sort of thing. Sometimes it’s a recipe I need for the church supper, or the folder I’m supposed to take to the doctor’s office, and I’ll be hunting all over the house for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things like appointments, conversations, or tasks you had planned to do, even if they’re written down?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I write things down, I’ll still miss them. I’ve forgotten to return calls, or I’ll find a bill sitting under a pile of mail that I meant to pay. I’ve forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s frustrating, because I really do mean to take care of these things.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you’ve been experiencing these difficulties with focus and organization? Have they been present for most of your life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been this bad for about a year and three months, I’d say. It really started causing trouble when my husband began needing regular help with his appointments and day-to-day arrangements. Before that, I managed better, but since then it’s been much harder to keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you, like your husband or family members, noticed changes in your memory or thinking abilities that concern them?\n\nPatient: Yes. My daughter has noticed, for sure. She’s started double-checking the bills and appointments because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep them all straight by myself anymore. And I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center, which was hard. I just couldn’t stay on top of all the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused or organized when you’re outside the house, like when you’re running errands, attending events, or talking with friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If I’m at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my attention just goes to them, even if I’m trying to write something down or plan something. Same thing at home if the television is on in the next room. I lose my place, and then I have to figure out what I was doing all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you can’t sit still for very long?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do notice that. Even when I’m trying to sit still and be polite in a meeting, I’ll find myself rubbing my hands, tapping my foot, clicking a pen, or shifting around in my chair. Sometimes I realize I’m doing it because people look over at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they’re talking, finishing their sentences, or acting on impulse without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as interrupting people a lot, but maybe I do it once in a while and don’t notice. Acting without thinking… I can’t say that’s a big thing I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have trouble staying focused or felt unusually restless or fidgety when you were a child, say before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember even when I was little, my teachers would say I was bright, but careless. They wrote that I forgot my books or homework, and I needed reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. So yes, it was there when I was a child.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a steady decline in your thinking or memory abilities over time, or do the difficulties come and go but have gotten worse recently?\n\nPatient: It’s not like I noticed a steady decline for years and years. It’s been this bad for about a year and three months now, since my husband started needing regular help with his appointments and just the day-to-day arrangements. That’s when it really started causing me a lot more trouble.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking, like trouble with judgment, problem-solving, or understanding visual information, such as getting lost in familiar places or having trouble recognizing objects?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I don’t think I’m getting lost in places I know, or having trouble recognizing things. Judgment or problem-solving… I’m not sure, but nothing stands out to me in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to follow through with tasks that require sustained mental effort, like reading a book, working on a puzzle, or planning something in detail?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, or even putting together a schedule for something, I’ll keep finding little reasons to put it off. It feels like my head is tired before I even start, so I just avoid it as long as I can.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your difficulties with focus and organization affect your daily routines, such as managing your time, starting tasks, or staying on track with responsibilities?\n\nPatient: It affects almost everything, honestly. I came in because I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and all these half-finished lists. I’ll sit down to try to sort it out, and I just don’t know which thing to do first. I haven’t had any testing like that, and no doctor has told me there’s been a decline in my memory or thinking beyond normal aging.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you have had trouble with focus and organization, and whether these problems have been continuous since childhood or started later in life?\n\nPatient: It’s been this bad for about a year and three months. I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. I’ll mean to sort through it, but then I get stuck, and before I know it, the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning or organizing tasks in other areas of your life, like managing your schedule or completing projects at work or home?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s mostly at home now, since I’m not working, but it shows up with anything I have to plan or sort out. I’ll have my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists all over the kitchen table. I sit down meaning to get it organized, and I just don’t know what to do first. Then I get stuck there, and somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused or organized in other parts of your life, like remembering conversations, keeping track of personal items, or following through with plans?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with keeping track of things. I’m forever looking for something I just had in my hand—my car keys, my reading glasses, appointment cards, that sort of thing. Sometimes it’s a recipe I need for the church supper, or the folder I’m supposed to take to the doctor’s office, and I’ll be hunting all over the house for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things like appointments, conversations, or tasks you had planned to do, even if they’re written down?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I write things down, I’ll still miss them. I’ve forgotten to return calls, or I’ll find a bill sitting under a pile of mail that I meant to pay. I’ve forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s frustrating, because I really do mean to take care of these things.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you’ve been experiencing these difficulties with focus and organization? Have they been present for most of your life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been this bad for about a year and three months, I’d say. It really started causing trouble when my husband began needing regular help with his appointments and day-to-day arrangements. Before that, I managed better, but since then it’s been much harder to keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you, like your husband or family members, noticed changes in your memory or thinking abilities that concern them?\n\nPatient: Yes. My daughter has noticed, for sure. She’s started double-checking the bills and appointments because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep them all straight by myself anymore. And I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center, which was hard. I just couldn’t stay on top of all the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused or organized when you’re outside the house, like when you’re running errands, attending events, or talking with friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If I’m at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my attention just goes to them, even if I’m trying to write something down or plan something. Same thing at home if the television is on in the next room. I lose my place, and then I have to figure out what I was doing all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you can’t sit still for very long?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do notice that. Even when I’m trying to sit still and be polite in a meeting, I’ll find myself rubbing my hands, tapping my foot, clicking a pen, or shifting around in my chair. Sometimes I realize I’m doing it because people look over at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they’re talking, finishing their sentences, or acting on impulse without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Acting without thinking… I can’t say that’s a big thing I’ve picked up on.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have trouble staying focused or felt unusually restless or fidgety when you were a child, say before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes. I remember even when I was little, my teachers would say I was bright, but careless. It’s been this bad for about a year and three months now, since my husband started needing regular help with his appointments and just the day-to-day arrangements. That’s when it really started causing me a lot more trouble.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking, like trouble with judgment, problem-solving, or understanding visual information, such as getting lost in familiar places or having trouble recognizing objects?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed anything like that. I don’t think I’m getting lost in places I know, or having trouble recognizing things. If there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, or even putting together a schedule for something, I’ll keep finding little reasons to put it off. It’s mostly at home now, since I’m not working, but it shows up with anything I have to plan or sort out. I’ll have my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and these half-finished lists all over the kitchen table. I sit down meaning to get it organized, and I just don’t know what to do first. Then I get stuck there, and somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused or organized in other parts of your life, like remembering conversations, keeping track of personal items, or following through with plans?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with keeping track of things. I’m forever looking for something I just had in my hand—my car keys, my reading glasses, appointment cards, that sort of thing. Sometimes it’s a recipe I need for the church supper, or the folder I’m supposed to take to the doctor’s office, and I’ll be hunting all over the house for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things like appointments, conversations, or tasks you had planned to do, even if they’re written down?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I write things down, I’ll still miss them. I’ve forgotten to return calls, or I’ll find a bill sitting under a pile of mail that I meant to pay. I’ve forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s frustrating, because I really do mean to take care of these things.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you’ve been experiencing these difficulties with focus and organization? Have they been present for most of your life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been this bad for about a year and three months, I’d say. It really started causing trouble when my husband began needing regular help with his appointments and day-to-day arrangements. Before that, I managed better, but since then it’s been much harder to keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you, like your husband or family members, noticed changes in your memory or thinking abilities that concern them?\n\nPatient: Yes. My daughter has noticed, for sure. She’s started double-checking the bills and appointments because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep them all straight by myself anymore. And I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center, which was hard. I just couldn’t stay on top of all the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused or organized when you’re outside the house, like when you’re running errands, attending events, or talking with friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If I’m at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my attention just goes to them, even if I’m trying to write something down or plan something. Same thing at home if the television is on in the next room. I lose my place, and then I have to figure out what I was doing all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or as though you can’t sit still for very long?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do notice that. I don’t think I’m getting lost in places I know, or having trouble recognizing things.
